The total onsite project area is 8.6 acres. Within this project area, the five

proposed building footprint areas will be 128,382 square feet comprising the

following: Phase I Building “A” 16,830 SF, Phase I Building “B” 30,600 SF,

Phase II Building “C” 30,600 SF, Phase II Building “D” 18,360, and Phase III

Building 31,992 SF. Considering the Phase III Building to potentially contain

four stories, the gross square footage of all buildings within this project will

be approximately 224,358 SF. The gross area of landscaping will be

approximately 53,945 square feet. The combined area of all asphalt and

concrete will be 192,289 square feet. The development of all phases will

require that each phase area is totally disturbed to remove an approximate

12” depth of topsoil, having an estimated combined volume of 13,875 CY

,

resulting in Phase I: 2.45 acres, 3,950 CY topsoil excavation, Phase II: 2.29

acres, 3,700 CY topsoil excavation, and Phase III: 3.86 acres, 6,230 CY topsoil

excavation. Some of the organic topsoil will be retained for landscaping. For

the proposed building and hard surfaced areas, approximately 52,855 cubic

yards of granular structural fill will be imported from a local source having

structural gravel fill. Completion of the onsite surfacing will include a

combined volume of crushed rock and asphalt on the order of 24,820 cubic

yards.

Individual Grading Permits are to be issued for each phase of development.

These plans will include all utility provisions, final gravel fill, and surfacing

improvements. Prior to applying for individual grading permits for each

phase, the applicant proposes the following pre-phase site development.

For initial maintenance of the project area, the sod is to be stripped and

disposed offsite. The underlying native organic topsoil will be stripped and

stockpiled for eventual fill in areas to be landscaped. Additional excavation of

non-organic silty/sandy soil will be performed to remove the native soils that

would otherwise displace into the project’s impervious drivelane section.

(approximately 12” gravel, 4”- 6” crushed rockand 3”- 4” HMA - 0.5 inch = 19

,

- 22 inches)

This temporary fill section will use a free-draining gravel that will allow direct

rainfall to soak into the ground thereby recognizing this initial fill as a

permeable non-restrictive layer. This temporary “strip and fill” will be used as

an equipment and material lay-down pad in support of each development

phase.

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 8 of 28

f. Could erosion occur as a result of clearing, construction, or use? If so, generally describe.

Yes, erosion may occur during periods when the surface vegetation has been

removed and the underlying soils are directly exposed to rainfall impacts.

During all phases of development, erosion and sediment controls will be

installed to retain silt and debris onsite and to allow filtered surface runoff

waters to exit the site. Once the site is surfaced with free-draining granular

structural fill materials, the potential for erosion will be significantly reduced,

however sediment controls will still remain in place until the majority of the site

is surfaced with hard materials.

g. About what percent of the site will be covered with impervious surfaces after project

construction (for example, asphalt or buildings)?

Within the total project area of 8.6 acres, the proposed buildings will have a

combined footprint area of 128,382 square feet (2.94-acres), which represents

34% of the overall site. Asphalt parking and drivelanes have a footprint area of

175,625 square feet (4.03-acres), which represents 47% of the overall site.

Concrete sidewalks and dumpster pads will have a footprint area of 16,664

square-feet (0.38-acres), which represents 4% of the overall site. In sum, 85% of

the total site will be covered by impervious surfaces, with the remaining area to

be landscaped.

h. Proposed measures to reduce or control erosion, or other impacts to the earth, if any:

Silt sacks installed under the grates of existing catch basins and under the

grates of new catch basins as they are installed will reduce any sediment from

being transported from the site. During the initial site clearing and filling phase,

it will be helpful for surface vegetation to remain as long as reasonably possible

so the underlying soils are not directly exposed to rainfall. Construction

equipment will be parked at a location where if there is a leak or spill, the

materials can be retained by spill-containment materials. At the onset of Phase

3 construction, the site area of Phase 1 and Phase 2 will have finished hard

surfaces and the building footprint area for Phase 3 will be covered with gravel.

Sediment controls will remain in place to trap and retain any sediment

transported by runoff waters.

In addition to traditional means of managing erosion controls, the underlying

soils have an infiltration capacity that is sufficient to handle most rainfall

events. Temporary ditches and depressed areas can manage rainfall onsite. As

construction proceeds, the location of temporary ditching and infiltration

ponds will be relocated so as to not obstruct construction activities. The

temporary facilities will function to manage rainfall directly on the site.

As previously stated, the applicant will likely propose stripping the site of

topsoil, and then applying 8”-12” thick layer of naturally-occurring free-draining

gravel until formal site development plans are approved. Because the gravel

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 9 of 28

material will be free-draining, and is capable of passing direct rainfall into the

ground, this initial layer of gravel will not be classified as a restrictive

impervious material.

2. AIR

a. What types of emissions to the air would result from the proposal during construction, operation,

and maintenance when the project is completed? If any, generally describe and give approximate

quantities if known.

During all phases of construction, a water source will be available during dry

weather periods to reduce the quantity of dust from trucking and gravel grading

activities. Construction equipment will be maintained for efficient operation.

Once the site is developed, emission sources will be from heating equipment

and vehicle exhaust. As of now, the type of future occupant is unknown,

howeverif business activities generate an emission, the building and

,

equipment design will be evaluated for having t to c control their emissions as part

of the building design process.

b. Are there any off-site sources of emissions or odor that may affect your proposal? If so,

generally describe.

No.

c. Proposed measures to reduce or control emissions or other impacts to air, if any:

Maintenance of all construction equipment during all 3 phases of development

will be required. Proper design and maintenance of equipment inside the

building will also be necessary.

3. WATER

a. Surface Water:

1) Is there any surface water body on or in the immediate vicinity of the site (including

year-round and seasonal streams, saltwater, lakes, ponds, wetlands)? If yes, describe type and

provide names. If appropriate, state what stream or river it flows into.

There are no water bodies, streams, or lakes in the immediate vicinity of the

project area. Per the Green Earth Wetland Assessment Report which has

been submitted to the City of Mount Vernon as a separate submittal item

associated with this SEPA, there is a Category III depressed wetland at the

northwest corner of the property. Upon receiving the necessary permits, this

depressed area will be filled with gravel. There is a an offsite fenced detention

pond on the adjacent property to the south. This pond will be unaffected by

any aspect of the Save On Storage development.

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 10 of 28

2) Will the project require any work over, in, or adjacent to (within 200 feet) the described

waters? If yes, please describe and attach available plans.

Yes, only the onsite depressed area which is intended to be filled.

3) Estimate the amount of fill and dredge material that would be placed in or removed

from surface water or wetlands and indicate the area of the site that would be affected.

Indicate the source of fill material.

Based on an average fill depth of 8 feet, the volume of imported fill (gravel)

for the 0.34 depressed area in the NW corner of the site will be

approximately 4,400 cubic yards. The source of the fill will be a permitted

gravel pit, conforming to MVMC 15.10.090(G)(8).

4) Will the proposal require surface water withdrawals or diversions? Give general

description, purpose, and approximate quantities if known.

No surface water withdrawals or diversions are anticipated during the

development and eventual operation of the site. Installation of sanitary

sewer pipe, which is the deepest utility being installed, will be

conducted/coordinated during seasonal times of lowest groundwater

elevation so no dewatering in necessary.

5) Does the proposal lie within a 100-year floodplain? If so, note location on the site plan.

Yes, this project lies within the 100-year floodplain. The property is located

in the Skagit River Flood zone having a base flood elevation of 30.0 thereby

requiring the lowest finished floor of all buildings to be at or above elevation

31.1. These flood zone elevations are based on the NGVD 1929 vertical

datum, and will be converted (+ 3.7’) to be comparable with this project’s

topographic survey performed on the NAVD 1988 vertical datum. As

represented on the accompanying Existing Conditions Plan, the native

ground surface elevation varies from 28-32, NAVD 1988.

6) Does the proposal involve any discharges of waste materials to surface waters? If so,

describe the type of waste and anticipated volume of discharge.

No. The development of the site and the activities within the proposed

buildings are not anticipated to generate any waste material discharge. The

site is served by public sanitary sewer and public domestic water service.

Protective measures will be in place and available during the site

development phases. If a building occupant proposes an activity that could

generate a potential waste product, the tenant design will be responsible for

addressing such matters to the satisfaction of the City. Rainfall from

pollution generating impervious surfaces that is infiltrated into the ground

is required to have “enhanced” treatment before being exposed to soaking

into the ground. No discharge of waste materials to surface waters is

anticipated.

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 11 of 28

b. Ground Water:

1) Will groundwater be withdrawn from a well for drinking water or other purposes? If so, give a

general description of the well, proposed uses and approximate quantities withdrawn from the

well. Will water be discharged to groundwater? Give general description, purpose, and

approximate quantities if known.

This project is served by a public water services, PUD. No permanent

ground water withdrawals are proposed. Runoff waters are intended to be

infiltrated into the ground in conformance with the 2019 DOE manual.

Runoff from all impervious and hard surfaces including roof, asphalt, and

concrete will be infiltrated into the ground. However, runoff from pollution

generating impervious surfaces (PGIS) will first be treated as required by

DOE, to protect the integrity of the underlying infiltration system and

groundwater quality. The approximate quantity of developed surface runoff

waters being infiltrated will be identified in the drainage summary report

which has been submitted to the City of Mount Vernon as a separate

submittal item associated with this SEPA

.

2) Describe waste material that will be discharged into the ground from septic tanks or

other sources, if any (for example: Domestic sewage; industrial, containing the

following chemicals. . . ; agricultural; etc.). Describe the general size of the system, the

number of such systems, the number of houses to be served (if applicable), or the number of

animals or humans the system(s) are expected to serve.

No sources of waste materials a are anticipated to discharged into the ground

during any phase of development of this site. The eventual occupancy of

the proposed buildings will require the occupants to not discharge any

waste material

.

c. Water runoff (including stormwater):

1) Describe the source of runoff (including storm water) and method of collection

and disposal, if any (include quantities, if known). Where will this water flow?

Will this water flow into other waters? If so, describe.

The vast majority of onsite developed surface runoff waters are to be

infiltrated onsite into the ground as identified in the drainage summary

report prepared for this project. The 2019 DOE drainage manual does allow

for a small offsite discharge of runoff waters, which will be incorporated into

this site’s drainage system design. An emergency overflow control structure

has been sized so that post-development, the site’s stormwater system will

not release runoff at rates that exceed pre-developed discharge durations

for the range of pre-developed discharge rates from 50% of the 2-year peak

flow up to the full 50-year peak flow as required by 2019 DOE standards.

This permissible quantity of runoff waters will be directed to the existing

storm drainage network w within Market Street to the east of the project.

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 12 of 28

2) Could waste materials enter ground or surface waters? If so, generally describe.

No.

3) Does the proposal alter or otherwise affect drainage patterns in the vicinity of the site? If so,

describe.

No. At the present time, the majority of rainfall upon the subject property

soaks into the ground. The intended function of the proposed onsite

stormwater management facilities is to treat specific runoff waters as

required, and to infiltrate nearly 100% of all developed runoff waters into the

ground onsite.

d. Proposed measures to reduce or control surface, ground, and runoff water, and drainage pattern

impacts, if any:

With the requirement to treat developed runoff waters from pollution

generating impervious surfaces, and to soak nearly 100% of all developed

runoff waters, there is minimal opportunity for this site development to

have a negative influence on groundwater and local conveyance facilities.

The development of a site does not cause an increase in rainfall, but rather,

it c changes how the rainfall is infiltrated into the ground. All s stormwater from

PGIS will receive “enhanced” treatment prior to being soaked into the

ground and prior to being discharged from the site in an emergency

overflow situation

.

2) Describe existing hazardous chemicals/conditions that might affect project development and

3) Describe any toxic or hazardous chemicals that might be stored, used, or produced during

the project's development or construction, or at any time during the operating life of the

project.

During all three phases of site development, which is predominately

limited to structural fill and utility infrastructure, the only potential

chemicals would be limited to onsite storage of fuel and liquids serving

construction equipment. Similar liquid needs will be necessary during

b. Noise

1) What types of noise exist in the area which may affect your project (for example:

traffic, equipment, operation, other)?

There are no noises in the vicinity that will negatively affect the

development or future business activities onsite. The only constant noise

factor would be traffic from I-5 located approximately 150-feet to the west

of the project.

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 16 of 28

2) What types and levels of noise would be created by or associated with the project on a

short-term or a long-term basis (for example: traffic, construction, operation, other)? Indicate

what hours noise would come from the site.

During all phases of development, which will take place over a couple of

years, there will be intermittent construction equipment such as

excavators, dump trucks, and vibratory rollers operating in the course of

stripping top soils, installing utilities, and placing structural fill materials.

These noises will be limited to day time work hours, commonly 7 am to 6

pm, Monday through Friday.

During phase 3 of development, for construction of the “U” shaped multi

level building, similar noises may be generated together with the operation

of small cranes depending on the height of the building and its building

materials.

After completion of the site development, daily business-related noises will

be generated from vehicles entering and exiting the site. For the

commercial buildings, it is likely that any manufacturing and light-industrial

activities will all be conducted indoors. Utilization of insulation not only for

temperature control but also for loud noise mitigation may be necessary

during the design phase of development.

3) Proposed measures to reduce or control noise impacts, if any:

Maintenance of construction equipment during the various stages of

construction activity during the site’s development.  Proper and wise

design of all buildings to reduce noise impacts.

8. LAND AND SHORELINE USE

a. What is the current use of the site and adjacent properties? Will the proposal affect current

land uses on nearby or adjacent properties? If so, describe.

Save On Storage LLC owns a total of four parcels encompassing 8.6 acres

adjoining the west side of Market Street in Mount Vernon, WA. The site is

currently undeveloped and consists of long unmaintained grass. The I-5

ROW parallels the west side of the overall property, and Market Street is

located to the east of the overall property. The property to the north of this

project is also owned by Save on Storage and is a mini storage commercial

facility. The property to the south of the project is the retail store Coastal

Farm and Ranch. This project will not affect current land uses on nearby or

adjacent properties.

many acres in farmland or forest land tax status will be converted to nonfarm or nonforest

Working forest lands have not existed in the Skagit Valley floodplain for

many decades. It is possible t that the subject property was involved in some

property’s use and zoning (C-2) suggest it hasn’t been farmland for many,

many years. Since there hasn’t been any agricultural activity on the subject

property on record, the proposed site development is not considered to be

converting land from an ag resource to a commercial resource. The subject

property is within the Mount Vernon City Limits and is zoned for

commercial activities.

11. LIGHT AND GLARE

a. What type of light or glare will the proposal produce? What time of day would it mainly

occur?

During all phases of development, 1-3, work will only be performed during

daylight hours, thereby not generating any evening light. After all buildings are

constructed and occupied, building-mounted lighting will be located on all 5

buildings to illuminate drivelanes, pedestrian paths, and parking areas. There

will also be pole-mounted lights to illuminate parking areas on the eastern half

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 20 of 28

of the project site. All building-mounted and pole-mounted lighting will be on

during all dark hours for security purposes, and will be shielded. There are no

surrounding residential developments to be affected by cast light glare.

b. Could light or glare from the finished project be a safety hazard or interfere with views?

No, because there are no prominate views surrounding the project area. All

lighting will be hooded and directed down onto drive lanes and parking areas.

Pole-mounted lighting will likely be located throughout the parking areas on the

eastern half of the site. Building-mounted lighting will not pose a safety hazard

or interfere with views.

c. What existing off-site sources of light or glare may affect your proposal?

None.

d. Proposed measures to reduce or control light and glare impacts, if any:

Hooded and shielded lights will reduce light and glare onto surrounding

properties. Illumination of parking areas for residents is necessary for safety

and security purposes. All surrounding properties are businesses, so there are

few people residing near the project site.

b. Are there any landmarks, features, or other evidence of Indian or historic use or occupation? T This

may include human burials or old cemeteries. Are there any material evidence, artifacts, or areas of

cultural importance on or near the site? Please list any professional studies conducted at the site to

identify such resources.

There is no known evidence of Indian or historic occupations on or surrounding

the site and none is known to have ever been encountered within the

surrounding commercial developments. A cultural resource report was

prepared by ERCI on June 25, 2025 for the project sitewhich has been

,

submitted to the City of Mount Vernon as a separate submittal item associated

with this SEPA. The ERCI team conducted a pedestrian and subsurface survey

within the Project area. No protected cultural resources were encountered.

c. Describe the methods used to assess the potential impacts to cultural and historic resources on or

near the project site. Examples include consultation with tribes and the department of archeology

and historic preservation, archaeological surveys, historic maps, GIS data, etc.

Fieldwork from ERCI’s team entailed a meandering pedestrian surface survey

and subsurface shovel probes. The technicians zig-zagged slowly up their

individual transects, pausing at alternating changes of direction to look

backwards at the ground surface. While surveying, in addition to the possibility

of surface artifacts, archaeologists were watching for surface features such as

cache pits, cultural depressions, wood building foundations and rock cairns.

d. Proposed measures to avoid, minimize, or compensate for loss, changes to, and disturbance to

resources. Please include plans for the above and any permits that may be required.

Providing that DAHP and the Tribes do not have any concerns for this project,

the following statement will be provided on the plans:

Inadvertent Discovery of Human Skeletal Remains on Non-Federal and Non

Tribal Land in the State of Washington (RCWs 68.50.645, 27.44.055, and

68.60.055)

“If ground disturbing activities encounter human skeletal remains during the

course of construction, then all activity will cease that may cause further

disturbance to those remains. The area of the find will be secured and protected

from further disturbance until the State provides notice to proceed. The finding

of human skeletal remains will be reported to the county medical

examiner/coroner and local law enforcement in the most expeditious manner

possible. The remains will not be touched, moved, or further disturbed. The

county medical examiner/coroner will assume jurisdiction over the human

skeletal remains and make a determination of whether those remains are

forensic or non-forensic. If the county medical examiner/coroner determines

the remains are non-forensic, then they will report that finding to the

Department of Archaeology and Historic Preservation (DAHP) who will then

take jurisdiction over the remains. The DAHP will notify any appropriate

cemeteries and all affected tribes of the find. The State Physical Anthropologist

SEPA Checklist  for Mount Vernon

Page 22 of 28

will make a determination of whether the remains are Indian or Non-Indian and

report that finding to any appropriate cemeteries and the affected tribes.  The

DAHP will then handle all consultation with the affected parties as to the future

preservation, excavation, and disposition of the remains."

The project area is bound by Market Street to the east and Interstate 5 (I-5) to

How many would the project or proposal eliminate?

The development of this C-2 zone will comply with the City’s permitted uses.

Based on current provisions in this zoning, the westerly half of this project,

Phase I and Phase II, will support general commercial, personal services,

automobile repairs, furniture repairs, and other similar business operations.

Because the applicant also owns the adjacent storage facility to the north,

Phase I and II of this project can also be developed in support of storage

facilities. Phase III development, also zoned C-2, can support larger

opportunities such as hotels, offices, restaurants, and retail businesses.

As represented on the Phasing Plan, 246 parking stalls are provided for the

overall project. Per the City of Mount Vernon Code, 232 parking stalls are

required. If more parking stalls are needed per code, or are found necessary for

an onsite use, additional parking can be provided. This project does not cause

the loss of any existing parking stalls.

Utilities including sanitary sewer, water for fire protection and domestic, fiber

optic/telephone/cable/power, and natural gas will be extended onto the property

from Market Street. Proposed utility extensions will serve all phases of

development.

A total of six onsite infiltration trenches will be provided to detain and infiltrate

runoff waters generated from all 3 phases of development. Each infiltration trench

will be comprised of clean, washed, ballast rock, and will be placed below the

proposed asphalt parking and drivelanes. The ballast rock will be placed below

the asphalt’s structural fill with a layer of separation fabric.

Wire, Fiber Optic, power & Natural Gas services will be designed to extend west

from Market Street to serve all 3 phases of development. Provisions will be made

for future connections and extensions during Phase 1 as needed for Phase 2 and

Phase 3. These services will be extended within a common joint utility trench.

Sanitary sewer gravity pipe from Market Street will be extended west onto the

connection will be included for Phase 2 and Phase 3 as needed. A total of 2

connections will be made to the subject property from the existing 12-inch sewer

main in Market Street.

and domestic water service for the initial two buildings. Within this waterline

extension, fitting provisions will be included to facilitate future water services to

the southerly two s southern mixed-use and storage buildings (aka Phase 2) as well

as the Phase 3 four-story building.

Subsurface Soil Conditions

Subsurface conditions were explored by advancing 12 test pits (TP-1 through TP-12) and 5 hand

auger boreholes (HA/MW-1 through HA/MW-5) across the subject area. The explorations were

advanced until either a target depth was reached or caving prevented further exploration. Soil

conditions were logged, and representative grab samples were collected according to industry

standards. Soil classification followed the guidelines of the American Society for Testing and

Materials (ASTM) D2488. Approximate locations of these explorations have been plotted on the

Site and Exploration Plan, Figure 2. A Soil Classification System and Key is presented as Figure 4.

Detailed exploration logs can be found as Appendix A - Exploration Logs.

Subsurface conditions were evaluated on November 7, 2023, by advancing TP-1 through TP-12

to depths of between 8.5 to 10 feet below ground surface (BGS) using a client-provided rubber

tracked excavator and operator, under the direction of a licensed geologist. The test pit

explorations encountered relatively consistent conditions, with generally a thin horizon of topsoil

overlying lightly or undisturbed native soils that extended to the termination depth of each test

pit.

Topsoil was encountered at all test pit locations and consisted of soft, dark brown, damp, sandy

silt with trace gravel, scattered rootlets that extended from to surface to approximate depths

ranging from 0.2 to 0.6 feet BGS. Within TP-2, uncontrolled fill was encountered underlying the

topsoil and extended to depths ranging from 0.3 and 5 feet BGS. The uncontrolled fill consisted

of very stiff, brown, moderately mottled, damp, gravelly silt with trace sand, roots, and topsoil

inclusions.

Underlying the topsoil and/or uncontrolled fill soil was generally medium stiff to very stiff, brown

to gray, slightly mottled, damp, very sandy silt, with trace gravel, trace rootlets, and woody debris

that was interpreted as weathered alluvium. The weathered alluvium extended to depths ranging

3

GeoTest Services, Inc.

May 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

from approximately 1 to 2.5 feet BGS and was not encountered within TP-2 and TP-10. Underlying

the weathered alluvium (where present) was unweathered alluvium that consisted of stiff, very

sandy silts and medium dense silty sand described as tan, moist, and contained trace gravel. The

native alluvium displayed similar characteristics throughout our explorations with the exception

of TP-10. Within TP-10, the alluvium was found to consist of soft gray moist, sandy, clayey silt

extending from approximately 3 to 7.5 feet BGS. At approximately 7.5 feet BGS, the alluvium

transitioned to a loose, gray, wet, coarse-grained sand. The alluvium was observed to extend to

the termination depths of the explorations.

Image 2 - Subsurface conditions within TP-4. Soils shown are typical of those  found throughout

the site. Photo taken on November 7, 2023.

Subsurface conditions were further explored by advancing 5 hand augers to facilitate

groundwater monitoring well installation. The explorations were advanced by GeoTest staff to

depths ranging from approximately 7.2 to 8.7 feet BGS using a hand operated soil auger. Hand

auger explorations encountered approximately 0.2 to 0.4 feet of topsoil overlying approximately

1.75 to 2.1 feet of weathered alluvium before transitioning to unweathered alluvium. Within HA

05, uncontrolled fill was encountered extending from the surface to an approximate depth of 2

feet BGS. The undcontrolled fill was described as stiff to very stiff, brown to gray, damp, slightly

gravelly silt. Underlying the weathered alluvium and uncontrolled fill was unweathered alluvium

that extended to the termination depths of the hand auger explorations.

Groundwater

At the time of our investigation, moderate groundwater seepage was encountered within TP-10

and HA/MW-4, located in the northwest corner of the site, at depths of 8.5 and 6 feet BGS,

respectively. The groundwater seepage observed during the explorations was interpreted to be

the regional groundwater table.

4

GeoTest Services, Inc.

May 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

The groundwater conditions reported on the exploration logs are for the specific locations and

dates indicated, and therefore may not be indicative of other locations and/or times.

Groundwater levels are variable and groundwater conditions fluctuate depending on local

subsurface conditions, precipitation, and changes in on-site and off-site use.

As part of our current scope of services, GeoTest conducted groundwater monitoring services

from early November of 2023 through mid-April of 2024 for the project. The results of our

groundwater monitoring are summarized below.

Groundwater Monitoring Study

The goal of our groundwater monitoring phase was to determine seasonal fluctuation and

thrd

associated high-water mark for the study period (November 6, 2023 through April 23, 2024).

th

Monitoring wells were constructed on November 6, 2023, and placed at locations indicated on

the attached Site and Exploration Plan, Figure 2.

Installation of 5 downhole automated piezometric data loggers was completed by GeoTest on

November 7, 2023, immediately following monitoring well construction (MW-1 through MW-5).

At all well locations, a two-inch SCH-40 PVC pipe was inserted to an approximate depth ranging

from 7.2 to 8.7 feet BGS. The bottom 5 feet consisted of slotted screen PVC to allow groundwater

flow. The outside of the well casing was packed with 20/40 Colorado silica filter sand, then topped

with bentonite clay chips in the upper foot. The well casing had a solid PVC cap and drain located

at the base. Monitoring wells included a “stick-up” height above the ground surface as well as

ventilation holes. Well construction and backfill was completed by GeoTest personnel in

accordance with industry standard techniques and materials.

At this time, GeoTest deployed In-Situ Rugged TROLL 100 data loggers into each well location. A

Rugged BaroTROLL surface barometric logger was also place near the northeast boundary of the

site. Data loggers were typically set a short distance above the base of the well and tied off to

the top cap with a steel cable to ensure a consistent level reference for data collection. The

downhole loggers were set to record downhole barometric pressure, temperature, and water

level above the loggers at 1-hour intervals throughout the monitoring period. The barometric

logger was set to record ambient pressure and temperature at 1-hour intervals.

To ensure functionality of the wells following the installation and prior to the deployment of data

loggers, existing well water was evacuated using an electric Everbuilt Non-submersible Transfer

Pump with a small diameter garden hose and powered by a portable Honda generator.

Monitoring wells were evacuated to the full depth 3 times, pausing to allow recharge for

approximately 5 minutes between pumping episodes. Commissioning of the wells produced

cleaner water return at the surface that was considered suitable for monitoring.

5

GeoTest Services, Inc.

May 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

An In-Situ Rugged BaroTROLL surface barometric logger was placed near the western boundary

of the existing gravel lot on the subject site. The barometric logger was set to record ambient

barometric pressure and temperature at 1-hour intervals.

Return visits to verify functionality of the downhole loggers and to download interim data were

thth

performed on December 6, 2023 and January 26, 2024. A final site visit to collect the downhole

rd

loggers and terminate the field study was performed on April 23, 2024. At the time of our last

visit the surface barometric logger was discovered to be missing. Therefore, barometric data from

a nearby site, collected over the same study period was utilized. The PVC wells were left in place

at the end of the study to facilitate further data collection, as needed.

After the data loggers were retrieved from the site, the recorded information was downloaded

and processed using the software program Win-Situ® Version 5.7.6.1 distributed by In-Situ Inc.©.

The ambient pressures recorded from the barometric loggers were used to correct the readings

from the downhole data loggers using the program Win-Situ® Baro Merge™ Version 1.4.5.1

distributed by In-Situ Inc.©. The barometric corrected values were used to determine the

groundwater elevation at each location throughout the monitoring period.

Project Number 23-2984

Based on a review of the above referenced documents, the subject site is located within lahar

flow paths of both Glacier Peak and Mount Baker. As such, the project area is considered to be

a Volcanic Hazard Area as defined by the MVMC.

Lahars are dense slurries of water-saturated debris that consist of rock, sand, soil, trees, and

other debris that moves down valley and looks and behaves much like flowing concrete. The

speed of a lahar debris flow depends on the steepness of the slope and the sediment load.

Average speeds range between 20 and 40 miles per hour and may reach as high as 65 miles per

hour. Debris flows may either be cohesive (high clay content – does not mix well with water) or

non-cohesive (low clay content – mixes with water to transform into watery floods). Lahars have

the potential to cause significant damage to public infrastructure and buildings, block and divert

river systems, and deposit multiple feet of sediment in a relatively short time.

As the proposed development is not considered a critical facility, the construction must meet the

city’s flood hazard regulations set forth in Chapter 15.36 of the MVMC.

Alluvial Fan Hazard Areas – MVMC 15.40.070(B5)

As the project site is not located in the vicinity of an alluvial fan, the site does not meet the

requirements set forth in this section. As such, the site is not considered an Alluvial Fan Hazard

Area as defined by MVMC and no specific mitigations for this hazard are required.

CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

Based on the evaluation of the data collected during this investigation, it is our opinion that the

subsurface conditions at the site are suitable for the proposed development, provided the

recommendations contained herein are incorporated into the project design.

GeoTest generally anticipates that approximately 0.5 foot of stripping will be needed to remove

near surface topsoil to expose the native alluvium soils below the development area. However,

deeper excavations of up to 5 feet may be required to reach suitable soils in the eastern margin

of the site where uncontrolled fill was encountered, within the vicinity of TP-2. Once undisturbed

native soils have been exposed, GeoTest recommends that the subgrade surface be recompacted

to a firm and unyielding condition with a smooth-drum roller or other appropriate piece of

construction equipment prior to placement of new structural fill. The subject site is rated as

having a “high” suseptability to liquefaction. However, the proposed development consists of

unoccupied storage units and, as such, liquefaction mitigations are considered outside the scope

of the project. At the Owner’s discretion, mitigations such as grade beams or structural slabs may

be considered to reduce the potential risks associated liquefaction induced differential

settlement of the proposed structures.

GeoTest Services, Inc.

May 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

GeoTest recommends that interior concrete slab-on-grade floors be underlain with at least 6

inches of clean, crushed, compacted, free-draining gravel. The free-draining gravel should

contain no fines. The purpose of this gravel layer is to provide uniform support for the slab,

provide a capillary break, and act as a drainage layer. We recommend the use of crushed, clean

¾-inch minus gravel as capillary break if intended to be used within the structural gravel base

section for slabs. To help reduce the potential for water vapor migration through floor slabs, a

continuous 10 to 15-mil minimum thick polyethylene sheet with tape-sealed joints should be

installed below the slab to serve as an impermeable vapor barrier. The vapor barrier should be

installed and sealed in accordance with the manufacturer’s instructions. American Concrete

Institute (ACI) guidelines suggest that the slab may be poured directly on the vapor barrier. If

moisture control within the building is critical, we recommend a representative of GeoTest

observe the vapor barrier to confirm that joints and penetrations have been properly sealed.

Exterior concrete slabs-on-grade, such as for parking and sidewalks, do not require capillary break

(clean, crushed gravel); however, long-term performance will be enhanced if exterior slabs are

placed on a layer of clean, durable, well-draining granular material as recommended herein.

A Subgrade Modulus (k) of 250 pounds per cubic inch (pci) is recommended for use in design of

concrete slab elements. This value is assuming site preparations prior to slab installation follow

the minimum soil preparation measures recommendation above, including recompaction of

existing native soil, as appropriated. This value assumes that a minimum of 12 inches of structural

fill, which can include quality capillary break, will underlie slab areas.

Foundation and Site Drainage

Positive surface gradients should be provided adjacent to the proposed building to direct surface

water away from the building and toward suitable drainage facilities. Roof drainage should not

be introduced into the perimeter footing drains but should be separately discharged directly to

the stormwater collection system or similar municipality-approved outlet. Pavement and

sidewalk areas, if present, should be sloped and drainage gradients should be maintained to carry

surface water away from the buildings towards an approved stormwater collection system.

Surface water should not be allowed to pond and soak into the ground surface near buildings or

paved areas during or after construction. Construction excavations should be sloped to drain to

sumps where water from seepage, rainfall, and runoff can be collected and pumped to a suitable

discharge facility.

To reduce the potential for groundwater and surface water to seep into interior crawl spaces,

GeoTest recommends that an exterior footing drain system be constructed around the perimeter

of new building foundations as shown in the Conceptual Footing and Wall Drain Section (Figure

3) of this report. The drain should consist of a perforated pipe measuring 4 inches in diameter at

minimum, surrounded by at least 12 inches of filtering media. The pipe should be sloped to carry

water to an approved collection system.

Utilities

Utility trenches must be properly backfilled and compacted to reduce cracking or localized loss

of foundation, slab, or pavement support. Excavations for new shallow underground utilities are

expected to be placed within either the native alluvium or imported structural fill.

Trench backfill in improved areas (beneath structures, pavements, sidewalks, etc.) should consist

of structural fill as defined in the Fill and Compaction section of this report. Outside of improved

areas, trench backfill may consist of reused native material provided the backfill can be

compacted to the project specifications. Trench backfill should be placed and compacted in

general accordance with the recommendations presented in the Fill and Compaction section of

this report.

Surcharge loads on trench support systems due to construction equipment, stockpiled material,

and vehicle traffic should be included in the design of any anticipated shoring system. The

contractor should implement measures to prevent surface water runoff from entering trenches

and excavations. In addition, vibration as a result of construction activity and traffic may cause

caving of the trench walls.

The contractor is responsible for trench configurations. All applicable local, state, and federal

safety codes should be followed. All open cuts should be monitored by the contractor during

excavation for any evidence of instability. If instability is detected, the contractor should flatten

the side slopes or install temporary shoring. If groundwater or groundwater seepage is present,

and the trench is not properly dewatered, the soil within the trench zone may be prone to caving,

channeling, and running. Trench widths may be substantially wider than under dewatered

conditions

.

Pavement Subgrade Preparation

The following recommendations are intended as typical pavement recommendations that may

be incorporated into roadways or access lanes expected to support the new development. The

final design will be performed by the civil engineer. We recommend stripping of all organic soil

and removing any existing fill to undisturbed native soil prior to the placement of the new

roadway fill. We anticipate pavements will be founded on a minimum of 18 inches of structural

fill due to the expected grading fill quantities. In addition, prior to the placement of road base

material, the exposed subgrade surface should be remedially compacted to firm and unyielding

condition. The following recommendations are meant as a guideline for the design engineer to

develop final pavement sections in accordance with current codes and standards.

Stormwater Infiltration Feasibility

It is our opinion that portions of the native alluvium underlying the site within the vicinity of TP

4, TP-5, TP-8, TP-9, and TP-11 may be suitable for onsite infiltration. A pervasive groundwater

table was encountered at TP-10 and HA/MW-4, located in the northwest corner of the site, at

depths of 8.5 and 6 feet BGS during our initial explorations in November of 2023. Additionally,

GeoTest previously performed a wet season groundwater monitoring study which determined

groundwater was not observed within 9 feet of the surface during 2023-2024 wet season

monitoring at the locations of MW-1, MW-2, and MW-5 while groundwater was recorded at 7.06

feet BGS at the location of MW-3 and above ground surface at MW-4. Per Site Suitability Criteria

(SSC), Volume 5, Section 5, of the Manual, Depth to Bedrock, Water Table or Impermeable Layer,

r,

proposed stormwater facilities require 5 feet of vertical separation between the seasonal

groundwater high and the bottom of an infiltration facility if the design does not account for

mounding. If a mounding analysis is performed, the vertical separation may be reduced to 3 feet,

per the Manual.

Provided adequate separation can be maintained from high groundwater elevations, we

recommend PIT testing be performed to determine infiltration rates once the location and

elevation of stormwater infiltration facilities have been determined.

Stormwater Treatment

The stormwater facilities on-site may require some form of pollutant pretreatment with an

amended soil prior to on-site infiltration or offsite discharge. The reuse of on-site topsoil is often

the most sustainable and cost-effective method for pollutant treatment purposes. Cation

exchange capacities, organic contents, and pH of site subsurface soils were also tested to

determine possible pollutant treatment suitability.

Cation exchange capacity, organic content, and pH tests were performed by Northwest

Agricultural Consultants on three soil samples collected from the test pit explorations. A

summary of the laboratory test results is presented in Table 3 below.

Save on Storage Development – PIT Addendum

Project Number: 23-2984

PURPOSE AND SCOPE OF SERVICES

The purpose of this evaluation is to establish general subsurface conditions beneath the site from

which conclusions and recommendations regarding stormwater infiltration for the project can be

formulated. Our scope of services includes the following tasks:

Perform two small-scale Pilot Infiltration Test (PIT-1 and PIT-2) on the project site within

the vicinity of the planned stormwater infiltration facilities. The PITs were performed in

general accordance with the 2019 Stormwater Management Manual for Western

Washington (SMMWW) as adopted by the City of Mount Vernon.

Perform laboratory testing on representative samples to classify and evaluate the

engineering characteristics of the soils encountered and to assess on-site infiltration

capability.

Provide a written addendum letter containing surface and subsurface condition

descriptions, long-term infiltration rates based on PIT results, and of our findings and

recommendations pertaining to stormwater design.

PROJECT DESCRIPTION

GeoTest previously performed a geotechnical evaluation for the subject property and proposed

development. The findings and conclusions from this evaluation were presented in the report

titled Geotechnical Engineering Report – Save on Storage Development. This report included

recommendations pertaining to site preparation and earthwork, foundation design, lateral earth

pressures, and other relevant geotechnical parameters pertinent to the design and construction

of the proposed buildings and facilities. The report also contained preliminary recommendations

pertaining to the feasibility of onsite infiltration of stormwater. These initial recommendations

included conducting Pilot Infiltration Testing to determine long-term infiltration rates that can be

used in the design of stormwater management facilities.

SITE CONDITIONS

This section includes a description of the general subsurface conditions observed at the project

site during the time of our field investigation. Interpretations of site conditions are based on the

results and review of available information, site reconnaissance, subsurface explorations,

laboratory testing, and previous experience in the project vicinity. There have been no significant

changes to the project site between our initial site visit on November 7, 2023.

Please reference the geotechnical engineering report for further details regarding the surface

conditions, general geologic conditions, and geologic hazard areas at the project site.

August 20, 2024

Save on Storage Development – PIT Addendum

Project Number: 23-2984

Subsurface Soil Conditions

Subsurface conditions within the area of interest were explored by excavating 2 Pilot Infiltration

Tests (PIT-1 and PIT-2) on August 1 and August 2, 2024, with an excavator and operator provided

by the client. The PIT explorations were initially advanced to depths of 3 and 3.7 feet below

ground surface (BGS) at PIT-1 and PIT-2, respectively. After the initial excavation, a PIT test, as

described below, was performed at the preplanned locations. Upon completion of the infiltration

tests, the explorations were advanced to the terminal depths of approximately 8 and 8.5 feet

BGS. Approximate location of the PIT tests and have been plotted on the Site and Exploration

Plan (Figure 2).

A GeoTest Licensed Geologist directed and observed excavation operations and logged the

conditions encountered. Representative grab samples were generally obtained at approximately

2-foot intervals, at the base of the PIT test, and changes in soil stratigraphy. Soils were classified

in general accordance with the guidelines of the American Society for Testing and Materials

(ASTM) D2487 and D2488. Upon completion, the PIT locations were backfilled to existing grades

with the excavation spoils and bucket tamped. A Soil Classification System and Key is found on

Figure 3. Detailed PIT exploration logs are attached as Figure 4, with laboratory test results are

presented as Figure 5.

Subsurface soil conditions consisted of a thin layer of topsoil that ranged from approximately 0.4

to 0.6 feet in thickness and was described as soft, dark brown, damp, sandy silt with trace gravel

and scattered rootlets. Underlying the topsoil soil was generally brown to gray, slightly mottled,

damp, very sandy silt and very silty sand, with trace rootlets that was interpreted as weathered

alluvium. The weathered alluvium soils were described as stiff and medium dense and extended

to depths ranging from approximately 1 to 1.5 feet BGS. Underlying the weathered alluvium was

unweathered alluvium that consisted of stiff silt and medium dense, very silty sand that were

described as tan and moist. The alluvium deposits observed within PIT-1 and PIT-2 were generally

consistent with those previously encountered during our initial exploration program.

Groundwater

Groundwater was not encountered within either PIT exploration. GeoTest previously conducted

a groundwater monitoring program at the site during the 2023/2024 wet season. Based on the

collected data, the peak groundwater elevations were recorded during late February and early

March 2024. At monitoring well locations MW-1, MW-2, and MW-5 groundwater levels remained

below the depth of the data loggers for the duration of the study period. Groundwater peaked

at 7.06 feet below ground surface at the MW-3 located on the southwest portion of the site.

MW-4 recorded a high groundwater elevation of 0.35 feet above ground surface and is

representative of groundwater ponded at the surface. The variation in recorded high-water

marks is likely due, in part, to varying surface elevation across the project site.

The subsurface water conditions reported herein are for the specific locations and date(s)

indicated, and therefore may not necessarily be indicative of other locations and/or times. Water

levels are not static, and it is anticipated that subsurface water conditions will vary seasonally,

annually, and with changes in land use at the site and vicinity.

CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

Based on the evaluation of the data collected during this investigation, it is our opinion that the

subsurface conditions at the site are suitable for the onsite infiltration of stormwater, provided

the recommendations contained herein are incorporated into the project design. The results

obtained from our performance infiltration testing and associated recommendations are

presented in the subsequent sections of this report.

Pilot Infiltration Testing

GeoTest performed two small-scale Pilot Infiltration Tests (PIT) on August 1 and August 2, 2024.

The tests were conducted in general accordance with the SMMWW to determine the initial

saturated hydraulic conductivity rate (Ksat initial). The base of both PIT locations was excavated

to the dimensions of approximately 16 square feet and to an approximate depth of 3 and 3.7 feet

BGS at PIT-1 and PIT-2, respectively. The approximate locations of the PIT tests are shown on the

Site and Exploration Plan, Figure 2.

Image 1 – PIT-1 in progress during the pre-soaking period. Photo taken on August 1, 2024

Infiltration testing was conducted by discharging water into a flat-bottom excavation for a 6-hour

“soaking period”. The purpose of the 6-hour pre-soak is to allow the soil in the immediate vicinity

of the test area to exhibit saturated conditions. GeoTest discharged water into the excavation at

a metered rate while keeping the water level within the testing area approximately fixed at a

depth of roughly 12 inches throughout the “soaking period”. The cumulative volume and

instantaneous flow rates were recorded approximately every 15 minutes. At the end of the 6-

Save on Storage Development – PIT Addendum

Project Number: 23-2984

Based on our PIT results, the alluvium deposits within the vicinity of the PIT test’s locations are

considered suitable for stormwater infiltration with a corrected design infiltration rate of 0.51

in/hr. The Civil Engineer should perform infiltration facility sizing based on this rate.

Results from PIT-2 indicate a potential infiltration rate of up to 2.04 in/hr. However, as the final

locations of infiltration facilities are unknown at the time of this report, and due to the variability

of the native alluvium underlying the site, we recommend preliminary infiltration facility sizing

be based on the corrected design infiltration rate of 0.51. Should an alternative infiltration rate

be pursued, we recommend additional confirmation explorations be conducted once final

stormwater facilities are determined. The provided rate assumes that adequate separation from

the regional groundwater table can be maintained by appropriate civil design.

In accordance with the SMMWW, the geotechnical engineer should observe the bottom of all

planned infiltration facilities during construction activities to confirm that the exposed soils are

as expected. The SMMWW recommends that a verification test be performed to confirm that

the measured field rate at the time of construction is equal to or greater than the infiltration rate

in the approved design. If the measured rates are lower than the design infiltration rate,

expansion of the facility and/or similar measures to increase storage may be required within the

footprint or adjacent to the constructed infiltration facility.

Geotechnical Consultation and Construction Monitoring

GeoTest recommends that we be involved in the project design review process. The purpose of

the review is to verify that the recommendations presented in this report are understood and

incorporated into the design and specifications.

We also recommend that geotechnical construction monitoring services be provided. These

services should include observation by GeoTest personnel during structural fill placement,

compaction activities, and subgrade preparation operations to confirm that design subgrade

conditions are obtained beneath the areas of improvement.

Periodic field density testing should be performed to verify that the appropriate degree of

compaction is obtained. The purpose of these services is to observe compliance with the design

concepts, specifications, and recommendations of this report. If subsurface conditions differ

from those anticipated before the start of construction, GeoTest would be pleased to provide

revised recommendations appropriate to the conditions revealed during construction.

GeoTest is available to provide a full range of materials testing and special inspection services

during construction as required by the local building department and the International Building

Code. This may include specific construction inspections on materials such as reinforced

concrete, reinforced masonry, wood framing, and structural steel. These services are supported

by our fully accredited materials testing laboratory.

Subsurface Soil Conditions

Subsurface conditions were explored by advancing 12 test pits (TP-1 through TP-12) and 5 hand

auger boreholes (HA/MW-1 through HA/MW-5) across the subject area. The explorations were

advanced until either a target depth was reached, or caving prevented further exploration. Soil

conditions were logged, and representative grab samples were collected according to industry

standards. Soil classification followed the guidelines of the American Society for Testing and

Materials (ASTM) D2488. Approximate locations of these explorations have been plotted on the

Site and Exploration Plan, Figure 2. A Soil Classification System and Key is presented as Figure 4.

Detailed exploration logs can be found as Appendix A - Exploration Logs.

Subsurface conditions were evaluated on November 7, 2023, by advancing TP-1 through TP-12

to depths of between 8.5 to 10 feet below ground surface (BGS) using a client-provided rubber

tracked excavator and operator, under the direction of a licensed geologist. The test pit

explorations encountered relatively consistent conditions, with generally a thin horizon of topsoil

overlying lightly or undisturbed native soils that extended to the termination depth of each test

pit.

Topsoil was encountered at all test pit locations and consisted of soft, dark brown, damp, sandy

silt with trace gravel, scattered rootlets that extended from to surface to approximate depths

ranging from 0.2 to 0.6 feet BGS. Within TP-2, uncontrolled fill was encountered underlying the

topsoil and extended to depths ranging from 0.3 and 5 feet BGS. The uncontrolled fill consisted

of very stiff, brown, moderately mottled, damp, gravelly silt with trace sand, roots, and topsoil

inclusions.

Underlying the topsoil and/or uncontrolled fill soil was generally medium stiff to very stiff, brown

to gray, slightly mottled, damp, very sandy silt, with trace gravel, trace rootlets, and woody debris

that was interpreted as weathered alluvium. The weathered alluvium extended to depths ranging

3

GeoTest Services, Inc.

July 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

from approximately 1 to 2.5 feet BGS and was not encountered within TP-2 and TP-10. Underlying

the weathered alluvium (where present) was unweathered alluvium that consisted of stiff, very

sandy silts and medium dense silty sand described as tan, moist, and contained trace gravel. The

native alluvium displayed similar characteristics throughout our explorations with the exception

of TP-10. Within TP-10, the alluvium was found to consist of soft gray moist, sandy, clayey silt

extending from approximately 3 to 7.5 feet BGS. At approximately 7.5 feet BGS, the alluvium

transitioned to a loose, gray, wet, coarse-grained sand. The alluvium was observed to extend to

the termination depths of the explorations.

Image 2 - Subsurface conditions within TP-4. Soils shown are typical of those found throughout

the site. Photo taken on November 7, 2023.

Subsurface conditions were further explored by advancing 5 hand augers to facilitate

groundwater monitoring well installation. The explorations were advanced by GeoTest staff to

depths ranging from approximately 7.2 to 8.7 feet BGS using a hand operated soil auger. Hand

auger explorations encountered approximately 0.2 to 0.4 feet of topsoil overlying approximately

1.75 to 2.1 feet of weathered alluvium before transitioning to unweathered alluvium. Within HA

05, uncontrolled fill was encountered extending from the surface to an approximate depth of 2

feet BGS. The undcontrolled fill was described as stiff to very stiff, brown to gray, damp, slightly

gravelly silt. Underlying the weathered alluvium and uncontrolled fill was unweathered alluvium

that extended to the termination depths of the hand auger explorations.

Groundwater

At the time of our investigation, moderate groundwater seepage was encountered within TP-10

and HA/MW-4, located in the northwest corner of the site, at depths of 8.5 and 6 feet BGS,

respectively. Groundwater was not observed within the remaining explorations. The two

explorations that observed groundwater were located in the northwest corner of the site, which

is several feet lower than the rest of the site. The groundwater seepage observed during the

explorations was interpreted to be the regional groundwater table.

GeoTest Services, Inc.

July 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

The groundwater conditions reported on the exploration logs are for the specific locations and

dates indicated, and therefore may not be indicative of other locations and/or times.

Groundwater levels are variable and groundwater conditions fluctuate depending on local

subsurface conditions, precipitation, and changes in on-site and off-site use.

As part of our current scope of services, GeoTest conducted groundwater monitoring services

from early November of 2023 through mid-April of 2024 for the project. The results of our

groundwater monitoring are summarized below.

Groundwater Monitoring Study

The goal of our groundwater monitoring phase was to determine seasonal fluctuation and

thrd

associated high-water mark for the study period (November 6, 2023 through April 23, 2024).

th

Monitoring wells were constructed on November 6, 2023, and placed at locations indicated on

the attached Site and Exploration Plan, Figure 2.

Installation of 5 downhole automated piezometric data loggers was completed by GeoTest on

November 7, 2023, immediately following monitoring well construction (MW-1 through MW-5).

At all well locations, a two-inch SCH-40 PVC pipe was inserted to an approximate depth ranging

from 7.2 to 8.7 feet BGS. The bottom 5 feet consisted of slotted screen PVC to allow groundwater

flow. The outside of the well casing was packed with 20/40 Colorado silica filter sand, then topped

with bentonite clay chips in the upper foot. The well casing had a solid PVC cap and drain located

at the base. Monitoring wells -

ventilation holes. Well construction and backfill was completed by GeoTest personnel in

accordance with industry standard techniques and materials.

At this time, GeoTest deployed In-Situ Rugged TROLL 100 data loggers into each well location. A

Rugged BaroTROLL surface barometric logger was also placed near the northeast boundary of

the site. Data loggers were typically set a short distance above the base of the well and tied off

to the top cap with a steel cable to ensure a consistent level reference for data collection. The

downhole loggers were set to record downhole barometric pressure, temperature, and water

level above the loggers at 1-hour intervals throughout the monitoring period. The barometric

logger was set to record ambient pressure and temperature at 1-hour intervals.

To ensure functionality of the wells following the installation and prior to the deployment of data

loggers, existing well water was evacuated using an electric Everbuilt Non-submersible Transfer

Pump with a small diameter garden hose and powered by a portable Honda generator.

Monitoring wells were evacuated to the full depth 3 times, pausing to allow recharge for

approximately 5 minutes between pumping episodes. Commissioning of the wells produced

cleaner water return at the surface that was considered suitable for monitoring.

5

GeoTest Services, Inc.

July 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

An In-Situ Rugged BaroTROLL surface barometric logger was placed near the western boundary

of the existing gravel lot on the subject site. The barometric logger was set to record ambient

barometric pressure and temperature at 1-hour intervals.

Return visits to verify functionality of the downhole loggers and to download interim data were

thth

performed on December 6, 2023 and January 26, 2024. A final site visit to collect the downhole

rd

loggers and terminate the field study was performed on April 23, 2024. At the time of our last

visit the surface barometric logger was discovered to be missing. Therefore, barometric data from

a nearby site, collected over the same study period was utilized. The PVC wells were left in place

at the end of the study to facilitate further data collection, as needed.

After the data loggers were retrieved from the site, the recorded information was downloaded

and processed using the software program Win-Situ® Version 5.7.6.1 distributed by In-Situ Inc.©.

The ambient pressures recorded from the barometric loggers were used to correct the readings

from the downhole data loggers using the program Win- Version 1.4.5.1

distributed by In-Situ Inc.©. The barometric corrected values were used to determine the

groundwater elevation at each location throughout the monitoring period.

GEOLOGIC HAZARD ASSESSMENT

According to Mount Vernon Municipal Code (MVMC) Section 15.40.070, Geologically Hazardous

Areas include erosion hazards, landslide hazards, seismic hazard, volcanic hazards, and alluvial

fan hazard areas. MVMC goes on to state that the purposes of the geologic hazard and hillside

development regulations are to:

1. Minimize damage due to landslide, erosion, subsidence, and alluvial fans through the

control of development; and

2. Reduce the risks to the city and its citizens from development occurring on unstable

slopes; and

3. Control erosion and sediment runoff from development.

Erosion Hazards MVMC 15.40.070(B1)

The MVMC defines Erosion Hazard Areas as areas that contain one or more of the following:

1. Those areas containing soils that, according to the U.S. Natural Resource Conservation

Service Survey, have severe to very severe erosion hazard potential; and/or

2. Those project areas that fall within any soil sloping greater than or equal to 30 percent;

and/or

8

GeoTest Services, Inc.

July 3, 2024

Save on Storage Development

Project Number 23-2984

3. Those areas that may be considered to have an erosion hazard as a result of rapid stream

incision or stream bank erosion.

Based on the NRCS Web Soil Survey, the on-site soils are classified as having a severe risk of

erosion. As such, the site is considered an erosion hazard area according to the MVMC. Long

term erosion may be mitigated through proper drainage and civil design. Stormwater volumes

generated from proposed impermeable surfaces should be collected and directed to a

municipally acceptable location. The following general recommendations are intended to

prevent excessive erosion from occurring at the site during and following construction:

All clearing and grading activities for future residence construction will need to

current Bellingham Municipal Codes and standards.

We recommend that appropriate silt fencing be incorporated into the construction plan

for erosion control.

Proper drainage controls have a significant effect on erosion. All collected stormwater

should be directed to an appropriate collection system.

All areas disturbed by the construction practices should be vegetated or otherwise

protected to limit the potential for erosion as soon as practical during and after

construction. Areas requiring immediate protection from the effects of erosion should be

covered with either plastic, mulch, or erosion control netting/blankets. Areas requiring

permanent stabilization should be seeded with an approved grass seed mixture,

hydroseeded with an approved seed-mulch-fertilizer mixture or landscaped with a

suitable planting design.

Landslide Hazards MVMC 15.40.070(B2)

The MVMC defines a variety of potential landslide hazard indicators. None of which are present

at the subject site. Coupled with the generally flat to gently sloping terrain throughout the

property, the site is not considered a landslide hazard area based on MVMC. Therefore, no

mitigation for this specific hazard is recommended.

damage as a result of earthquake induced ground shaking, slope failure, settlement, soil

liquefaction or surface faulting as. Areas that have a potential for soil liquefaction and soil

strength loss during ground shaking as identified on the city of Mount Vernon Soil Liquefaction

Project Number 23-2984

Based on a review of the above referenced documents, the subject site is located within lahar

flow paths of both Glacier Peak and Mount Baker. As such, the project area is considered to be

a Volcanic Hazard Area as defined by the MVMC.

Lahars are dense slurries of water-saturated debris that consist of rock, sand, soil, trees, and

other debris that moves down valley and looks and behaves much like flowing concrete. The

speed of a lahar debris flow depends on the steepness of the slope and the sediment load.

Average speeds range between 20 and 40 miles per hour and may reach as high as 65 miles per

hour. Debris flows may either be cohesive (high clay content does not mix well with water) or

non-cohesive (low clay content mixes with water to transform into watery floods). Lahars have

the potential to cause significant damage to public infrastructure and buildings, block and divert

river systems, and deposit multiple feet of sediment in a relatively short time.

As the proposed development is not considered a critical facility, the construction must meet the

Alluvial Fan Hazard Areas MVMC 15.40.070(B5)

As the project site is not located in the vicinity of an alluvial fan, the site does not meet the

requirements set forth in this section. As such, the site is not considered an Alluvial Fan Hazard

Area as defined by MVMC and no specific mitigations for this hazard are required.

CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

Based on the evaluation of the data collected during this investigation, it is our opinion that the

subsurface conditions at the site are suitable for the proposed development, provided the

recommendations contained herein are incorporated into the project design.

GeoTest generally anticipates that approximately 0.5 foot of stripping will be needed to remove

near surface topsoil to expose the native alluvium soils below the development area. However,

deeper excavations of up to 5 feet may be required to reach suitable soils in the eastern margin

of the site where uncontrolled fill was encountered, within the vicinity of TP-2. Once undisturbed

native soils have been exposed, GeoTest recommends that the subgrade surface be recompacted

to a firm and unyielding condition with a smooth-drum roller or other appropriate piece of

construction equipment prior to placement of new structural fill. The subject site is rated as

sumps where water from seepage, rainfall, and runoff can be collected and pumped to a suitable

To reduce the potential for groundwater and surface water to seep into interior crawl spaces,

GeoTest recommends that an exterior footing drain system be constructed around the perimeter

of new building foundations as shown in the Conceptual Footing and Wall Drain Section (Figure

3) of this report. The drain should consist of a perforated pipe measuring 4 inches in diameter at

minimum, surrounded by at least 12 inches of filtering media. The pipe should be sloped to carry

water to an approved collection system.

The filtering media may consist of open-graded drain rock wrapped in a nonwoven geotextile

fabric such as Mirafi 140N (or equivalent) or wrapped with a graded sand and gravel

filter. For foundations supporting retaining walls, drainage backfill should be carried up the back

of the wall and be at least 12 inches wide. The drainage backfill should extend from the

foundation drain to within approximately one foot of the finished grade and consist of open

graded drain rock containing less than 3 percent fines by weight passing the U.S. Standard No.

200 sieve (based on a wet sieve analysis of that portion passing the U.S. Standard No. 4 sieve).

The invert of the footing drainpipe should be placed at approximately the same elevation as the

bottom of the footing so that water will be collected. The drain system should include cleanouts

to allow for periodic maintenance and inspection.

Please understand that the above recommendations are intended to assist the design engineer

and/or architect in development of foundation and site drainage parameters and are based on

our experience with similar projects in the area. The final foundation and site drainage plan that

will be incorporated into the project plans is to be determined by the design team.

Resistance to Lateral Loads

The lateral earth pressures that develop against foundations and/or retaining walls will depend

on the method of backfill placement, degree of compaction, slope of backfill, type of backfill

material, provisions for drainage, magnitude and location of any adjacent surcharge loads, and

the degree to which the wall can yield laterally during or after placement of backfill. If the wall is

allowed to rotate or yield so the top of the wall moves an amount equal to or greater than about

0.001 to 0.002 times its height (a yielding wall), the soil pressure exerted comprises the active

soil pressure. When a wall is restrained against lateral movement or tilting (a nonyielding wall),

the soil pressure exerted comprises the at rest soil pressure. Wall restraint may develop if a rigid

structural network is constructed prior to backfilling or if the wall is inherently stiff.

GeoTest recommends that yielding walls under drained conditions be designed for an equivalent

fluid density of 35 pounds per cubic foot (pcf), for structural fill in active soil conditions.

Nonyielding walls under drained conditions should be designed for an equivalent fluid density of

55 pcf, for structural fill in at-rest conditions. Design of walls should include appropriate lateral

pressures caused by surcharge loads located within a horizontal distance equal to or less than

may be sloped as steep as 1.5H:1V (Horizontal: Vertical). All soils encountered are classified as

Type C soil in the presence of groundwater seepage. Flatter slopes or temporary shoring may be

required in areas where groundwater flow is present and unstable conditions develop; however,

groundwater was not encountered during site explorations. Temporary slopes and excavations

should be protected as soon as possible using appropriate methods to prevent erosion from

occurring during periods of wet weather.

GeoTest recommends that permanent cut or fill slopes be designed for inclinations of 2H:1V or

flatter. Permanent cuts or fills used in detention ponds, retention ponds, or earth slopes intended

to hold water should be 3H:1V or flatter. All permanent slopes should be vegetated or otherwise

protected to limit the potential for erosion as soon as practical after construction.

Utilities

Utility trenches must be properly backfilled and compacted to reduce cracking or localized loss

of foundation, slab, or pavement support. Excavations for new shallow underground utilities are

expected to be placed within either the native alluvium or imported structural fill.

Trench backfill in improved areas (beneath structures, pavements, sidewalks, etc.) should consist

of structural fill as defined in the Fill and Compaction section of this report. Outside of improved

areas, trench backfill may consist of reused native material provided the backfill can be

compacted to the project specifications. Trench backfill should be placed and compacted in

general accordance with the recommendations presented in the Fill and Compaction section of

this report.

Surcharge loads on trench support systems due to construction equipment, stockpiled material,

and vehicle traffic should be included in the design of any anticipated shoring system. The

contractor should implement measures to prevent surface water runoff from entering trenches

and excavations. In addition, vibration as a result of construction activity and traffic may cause

caving of the trench walls.

The contractor is responsible for trench configurations. All applicable local, state, and federal

safety codes should be followed. All open cuts should be monitored by the contractor during

excavation for any evidence of instability. If instability is detected, the contractor should flatten

the side slopes or install temporary shoring. If groundwater or groundwater seepage is present,

and the trench is not properly dewatered, the soil within the trench zone may be prone to caving,

channeling, and running. Trench widths may be substantially wider than under dewatered

conditions

.

Pavement Subgrade Preparation

The following recommendations are intended as typical pavement recommendations that may

be incorporated into roadways or access lanes expected to support the new development. The

21
